========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hey, yeah, good to meet you too. Thanks for fitting me in. So, look, I’m not usually the type of guy to come sit in a doctor's office, you know? I’ve always just powered through things. I mean, I didn’t even finish high school, just got straight to work, built my business from the ground up, and now I’m the CEO. So I'm used to handling a lot on my plate and just dealing with it. 

But what made me finally come in today is that... well, for the past year or so, it’s just been getting ridiculous. The absolute smallest things are throwing me off, and it's driving me crazy. I'll be sitting at my desk trying to review something important, and my phone will give that tiny little half-second buzz—not even a call, just a buzz from some useless app notification—and boom, my focus is completely gone. Or, you know, my office has these big glass walls. I thought it was a great idea for company culture, transparency, all that. But now, if somebody just walks past my office heading to the breakroom, I’m watching them, wondering what they're working on, and I've completely lost my place. 

And it’s not even just stuff happening around me, either. It’s my own head. I’ll be sitting there, trying to work, and suddenly I’ll have this totally random thought about my in-laws—like, some offhand comment my wife's dad made at a barbecue three weeks ago—and before I can even stop myself, I’m off down this mental rabbit hole and completely pulled off what I was doing. It just rips me right out of my work before I can even hit the brakes, and I just can't seem to get a handle on it lately.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
You know, it’s funny you ask that, because... well, I haven't thought about my school days in a long time. Like I said, I got out of there as soon as I could and just started working. But looking back... yeah. Yeah, this actually was around when I was a kid, too. I mean, it was a whole thing. My teachers were just constantly, and I mean constantly, moving my seat. It was like a game of musical chairs but just for me. They’d put me in the back, then I’d be too distracting, so they’d move me right up to the front, literally touching the teacher's desk, thinking that would fix it. 

But it didn't really matter where they put me, because I just... I talked way too much. I'd talk to the kid next to me, the kid behind me, whoever would listen, really. I just always had something to say. And I could never just stay put. I'd just get up without asking. I wouldn't even think about it, I'd just suddenly be out of my chair walking to the pencil sharpener or going to look at something on the bulletin board while the teacher was right in the middle of a lesson. They hated that. 

And the other thing that drove them absolutely up the wall was that I would just blurt things out. A teacher would be standing there, halfway through asking a question to the class, setting up a whole math problem or something, and before they could even finish getting the words out, I was already shouting out the answer. Or just a comment. Whatever popped into my head. It was like I couldn't hold it in for even a second to let them finish the sentence. So... yeah, I guess the more I think about it, I was definitely a handful back then with all that. It just felt like I had this motor running that I couldn't shut off.

It’s just... it’s exhausting, honestly. It’s like the volume on all the distractions just got cranked all the way up right around that time, and I just haven't been able to tune any of it out since. No, no, I wouldn't say I'm a worrier in general. I mean, look, as a CEO, you have to anticipate problems, right? You have to look down the road and see where the potholes are. So sure, I think about supply chain issues, or whether a competitor is going to undercut us, or if inflation is going to mess with our margins. But that's just strategic planning. That's just part of the job. It's not like I'm sitting around biting my nails about every little thing in the world. 

The in-law thing, though, that's different. That's personal, and it's in my house, and it affects my wife, so yeah, that takes up a lot of mental real estate. But outside of that specific mess, and just the normal day-to-day pressure of running a company, I'm not really losing sleep over a bunch of random other things. I don't sit there worrying about my health or the news or if I left the stove on or whatever. I'm too busy for that kind of worry, honestly. I've got a business to run and a team of forty people relying on me to make payroll, so my focus—when I can actually get it to cooperate—is pretty much locked on the immediate fires I need to put out, not just free-floating anxiety about everything under the sun.

that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, all the time. It's actually a huge problem, especially when I'm not the one running the show. Like, if I'm at work pacing around the boardroom, that's fine, it looks dynamic, right? But put me in a situation where I'm just supposed to sit there and be quiet? Forget it. 

Like, take family dinners, for example. My wife loves these big, sit-down Sunday dinners. We get the nice plates out, the whole nine yards. And I love my family, I really do, but sitting there for an hour, an hour and a half, just making small talk while we eat roast chicken... it's torture. Honestly, it feels like there is this actual, physical motor running inside my chest, just humming away, telling me to get up, move, do something. I end up bouncing my leg under the table so hard I shake the glassware, or I'm constantly getting up to refill water glasses or clear plates before people are even done eating just so I have an excuse to be on my feet. 

And it's not just at home, either. I got roped into being on this volunteer committee for our church—which, don't even get me started on how inefficiently those meetings are run. We sit in these little folding chairs in the basement for two hours every Tuesday night, going over the budget for the bake sale or whatever. And I'm sitting there, and I literally cannot keep my hands still. I'm clicking my pen until the guy next to me gives me a look, or I'm tearing my styrofoam coffee cup into tiny little pieces, or I'm checking my phone under the table. I just have this overwhelming urge to be doing something, anything, other than just sitting there. It's like my body physically rejects the idea of being stationary for more than ten minutes unless I'm dead asleep.

They hated that. \n\nAnd the other thing that drove them absolutely up the wall was that I would just blurt things out. A teacher would be standing there, halfway through asking a question to the class, setting up a whole math problem or something, and before they could even finish getting the words out, I was already shouting out the answer. Or just a comment. Whatever popped into my head. It was like I couldn't hold it in for even a second to let them finish the sentence. So... yeah, I guess the more I think about it, I was definitely a handful back then with all that. It just felt like I had this motor running that I couldn't shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several days when you felt unusually energized or wired and were much more active than usual?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I'm a high-energy guy, right? You don't build a company from nothing without having some drive. I'm always on the go, always thinking about the next quarter, the next product launch, dealing with vendors, managing my team... it's just non-stop. So I guess you could say I'm always a little wired, just by the nature of the job. But not in some weird, unusual way that lasts for days where I'm suddenly, I don't know, bouncing off the walls more than my normal baseline. \n\nLike, last week, we had this massive pitch to a new investor group, and yeah, I was pumped up. I stayed late at the office three nights in a row, drinking probably way too much espresso, just tweaking the slide deck and making sure every single financial projection was bulletproof. My wife kept texting me, asking when I was coming home, and I just couldn't pull myself away because I had that adrenaline going. But that's just business, you know? That's just doing what it takes to close the deal. It wasn't some strange phase where I felt completely out of control or anything like that. Once the pitch was over—we nailed it, by the way—I went right back to my normal level of busy. So, no, nothing like what you're describing, just the usual hustle.\n\nDoctor: Did these worsening focus problems start after a particular stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, actually, now that you mention it... remember how I brought up my father-in-law earlier? The whole barbecue thing? Yeah, so, it’s not just a one-off comment. It’s been this whole massive, ongoing ordeal. I'd say this whole thing—my focus just being completely shot and noticeably harder to manage—it's been going on for about a year and two months now. \n\nAnd I know exactly how long it's been because that’s exactly when this huge conflict with my in-laws really started blowing up and just completely taking over our family life. Like, they've always been a little demanding, sure, always had opinions, but a little over a year ago, it just escalated. It’s just... it’s exhausting, honestly. It’s like the volume on all the distractions just got cranked all the way up right around that time, and I just haven't been able to tune any of it out since. It's just bleeding into my work, my headspace, everything.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, have you been worrying excessively about lots of different things, not just the conflict with your in-laws?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I'm a worrier in general. I've got a business to run and a team of forty people relying on me to make payroll, so my focus—when I can actually get it to cooperate—is pretty much locked on the immediate fires I need to put out, not just free-floating anxiety about everything under the sun.\n\nDoctor: Even now as an adult, do you often feel restless, like you have a hard time sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, all the time. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Emotional distress? I mean... no, I wouldn't use that word. "Distress" makes it sound like I'm, I don't know, sitting in the dark crying about it or something. It’s not like that at all. It’s more just... extreme, mind-numbing frustration. 

Because, look, I’m not heartbroken that my father-in-law doesn't get me. The guy worked a comfortable middle-management job for thirty-five years, punched a clock, went home. Which is fine! Respectable. But he has absolutely zero concept of what it takes to build a company from nothing. I mean, I left high school in the eleventh grade. I didn't have a safety net, I didn't have a diploma to fall back on. I had to hustle, I had to fight for every single inch of ground I have to get to where I am today. So I don't exactly value his opinion on my work-life balance, you know? Emotionally, his approval means absolutely nothing to me. I'm not distressed by the lack of it. 

But what it *does* do is create this incredibly annoying, constant friction. It’s like... it's like a mosquito buzzing right in your ear when you're trying to read a complicated contract. You aren't emotionally devastated by the mosquito, right? You’re just intensely irritated because it won't go away and it’s completely breaking your concentration. That’s what this is. I just get so fed up with the sheer audacity of them coming into my house, drinking my wine, and making these passive-aggressive little jabs about how I'm on my phone too much. 

My wife gets emotional about it, sure, because they're her parents, and I hate seeing her upset. That part bothers me, obviously, because I'm protective of her. But for me personally? No deep emotional trauma here. I'm not depressed over it. I just want the noise to stop. I just want to be able to focus on the things that actually matter, like scaling my business and providing for my family, without this constant, ridiculous peanut gallery chiming in every five minutes and completely derailing my train of thought. So yeah, annoyance? Off the charts. But emotional distress? No, definitely not.

It just rips me right out of my work before I can even hit the brakes, and I just can't seem to get a handle on it lately.\n\nDoctor: Before the past year, did you have similar trouble focusing when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: You know, it’s funny you ask that, because... well, I haven't thought about my school days in a long time. Like I said, I got out of there as soon as I could and just started working. But looking back... yeah. Yeah, this actually was around when I was a kid, too. I mean, it was a whole thing. My teachers were just constantly, and I mean constantly, moving my seat. It was like a game of musical chairs but just for me. They’d put me in the back, then I’d be too distracting, so they’d move me right up to the front, literally touching the teacher's desk, thinking that would fix it. \n\nBut it didn't really matter where they put me, because I just... I talked way too much. I'd talk to the kid next to me, the kid behind me, whoever would listen, really. I just always had something to say. And I could never just stay put. I'd just get up without asking. I wouldn't even think about it, I'd just suddenly be out of my chair walking to the pencil sharpener or going to look at something on the bulletin board while the teacher was right in the middle of a lesson. They hated that. \n\nAnd the other thing that drove them absolutely up the wall was that I would just blurt things out. A teacher would be standing there, halfway through asking a question to the class, setting up a whole math problem or something, and before they could even finish getting the words out, I was already shouting out the answer. Or just a comment. Whatever popped into my head. It was like I couldn't hold it in for even a second to let them finish the sentence. So... yeah, I guess the more I think about it, I was definitely a handful back then with all that. It just felt like I had this motor running that I couldn't shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several days when you felt unusually energized or wired and were much more active than usual?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I'm a high-energy guy, right? You don't build a company from nothing without having some drive. I'm always on the go, always thinking about the next quarter, the next product launch, dealing with vendors, managing my team... it's just non-stop. So I guess you could say I'm always a little wired, just by the nature of the job. But not in some weird, unusual way that lasts for days where I'm suddenly, I don't know, bouncing off the walls more than my normal baseline. \n\nLike, last week, we had this massive pitch to a new investor group, and yeah, I was pumped up. I stayed late at the office three nights in a row, drinking probably way too much espresso, just tweaking the slide deck and making sure every single financial projection was bulletproof. My wife kept texting me, asking when I was coming home, and I just couldn't pull myself away because I had that adrenaline going. But that's just business, you know? That's just doing what it takes to close the deal. It wasn't some strange phase where I felt completely out of control or anything like that. Once the pitch was over—we nailed it, by the way—I went right back to my normal level of busy. So, no, nothing like what you're describing, just the usual hustle.\n\nDoctor: Did these worsening focus problems start after a particular stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, actually, now that you mention it... remember how I brought up my father-in-law earlier? The whole barbecue thing? Yeah, so, it’s not just a one-off comment. It’s been this whole massive, ongoing ordeal. I'd say this whole thing—my focus just being completely shot and noticeably harder to manage—it's been going on for about a year and two months now. \n\nAnd I know exactly how long it's been because that’s exactly when this huge conflict with my in-laws really started blowing up and just completely taking over our family life. Like, they've always been a little demanding, sure, always had opinions, but a little over a year ago, it just escalated. They started getting way too involved in how my wife and I are running our household, questioning my work hours, making these little passive-aggressive comments about how much time I spend at the office. Which, by the way, you don't become a CEO by working nine to five, and I've tried explaining this to them a hundred times, but it just doesn't compute. \n\nSo it created this constant, heavy tension. My wife is stressed out about it, which obviously stresses me out, and then I'll be sitting at my desk trying to look at a P&L statement, and instead of doing my job, my brain is just suddenly replaying some stupid argument we had in their driveway fourteen months ago. It’s just... it’s exhausting, honestly. It’s like the volume on all the distractions just got cranked all the way up right around that time, and I just haven't been able to tune any of it out since. It's just bleeding into my work, my headspace, everything.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, have you been worrying excessively about lots of different things, not just the conflict with your in-laws?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I'm a worrier in general. I mean, look, as a CEO, you have to anticipate problems, right? You have to look down the road and see where the potholes are. So sure, I think about supply chain issues, or whether a competitor is going to undercut us, or if inflation is going to mess with our margins. But that's just strategic planning. That's just part of the job. It's not like I'm sitting around biting my nails about every little thing in the world. \n\nThe in-law thing, though, that's different. That's personal, and it's in my house, and it affects my wife, so yeah, that takes up a lot of mental real estate. But outside of that specific mess, and just the normal day-to-day pressure of running a company, I'm not really losing sleep over a bunch of random other things. I don't sit there worrying about my health or the news or if I left the stove on or whatever. I'm too busy for that kind of worry, honestly. I've got a business to run and a team of forty people relying on me to make payroll, so my focus—when I can actually get it to cooperate—is pretty much locked on the immediate fires I need to put out, not just free-floating anxiety about everything under the sun.\n\nDoctor: Even now as an adult, do you often feel restless, like you have a hard time sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, all the time. It's actually a huge problem, especially when I'm not the one running the show. Like, if I'm at work pacing around the boardroom, that's fine, it looks dynamic, right? But put me in a situation where I'm just supposed to sit there and be quiet? Forget it. \n\nLike, take family dinners, for example. My wife loves these big, sit-down Sunday dinners. We get the nice plates out, the whole nine yards. And I love my family, I really do, but sitting there for an hour, an hour and a half, just making small talk while we eat roast chicken... it's torture. Honestly, it feels like there is this actual, physical motor running inside my chest, just humming away, telling me to get up, move, do something. I end up bouncing my leg under the table so hard I shake the glassware, or I'm constantly getting up to refill water glasses or clear plates before people are even done eating just so I have an excuse to be on my feet. \n\nAnd it's not just at home, either. I got roped into being on this volunteer committee for our church—which, don't even get me started on how inefficiently those meetings are run. We sit in these little folding chairs in the basement for two hours every Tuesday night, going over the budget for the bake sale or whatever. And I'm sitting there, and I literally cannot keep my hands still. I'm clicking my pen until the guy next to me gives me a look, or I'm tearing my styrofoam coffee cup into tiny little pieces, or I'm checking my phone under the table. I just have this overwhelming urge to be doing something, anything, other than just sitting there. It's like my body physically rejects the idea of being stationary for more than ten minutes unless I'm dead asleep.\n\nDoctor: Has the conflict with your in-laws been causing you a lot of emotional distress?\n\nPatient: Emotional distress? I mean... no, I wouldn't use that word. \"Distress\" makes it sound like I'm, I don't know, sitting in the dark crying about it or something. It’s not like that at all. It’s more just... extreme, mind-numbing frustration. \n\nBecause, look, I’m not heartbroken that my father-in-law doesn't get me. The guy worked a comfortable middle-management job for thirty-five years, punched a clock, went home. Which is fine! Respectable. But he has absolutely zero concept of what it takes to build a company from nothing. I mean, I left high school in the eleventh grade. I didn't have a safety net, I didn't have a diploma to fall back on. I had to hustle, I had to fight for every single inch of ground I have to get to where I am today. So I don't exactly value his opinion on my work-life balance, you know? Emotionally, his approval means absolutely nothing to me. I'm not distressed by the lack of it. \n\nBut what it *does* do is create this incredibly annoying, constant friction. It’s like... it's like a mosquito buzzing right in your ear when you're trying to read a complicated contract. You aren't emotionally devastated by the mosquito, right? You’re just intensely irritated because it won't go away and it’s completely breaking your concentration. That’s what this is. I just get so fed up with the sheer audacity of them coming into my house, drinking my wine, and making these passive-aggressive little jabs about how I'm on my phone too much. \n\nMy wife gets emotional about it, sure, because they're her parents, and I hate seeing her upset. That part bothers me, obviously, because I'm protective of her. But for me personally? No deep emotional trauma here. I'm not depressed over it. I just want the noise to stop. I just want to be able to focus on the things that actually matter, like scaling my business and providing for my family, without this constant, ridiculous peanut gallery chiming in every five minutes and completely derailing my train of thought. So yeah, annoyance? Off the charts. But emotional distress? No, definitely not.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. (Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n[S013] Quiet_Activity_Difficulty: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n[S014] Constant_Activity: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n[S015] Excessive_Talking: Often talks excessively.\n[S016] Blurting_Answers: Often blurts out an answer before a question has been completed.\n[S017] Waiting_Turn_Difficulty: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n[S018] Interrupting_Intruding: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n[S019] Delusions: Fixed beliefs that do not change easily, even in the face of contradictory evidence.  Subtypes: Persecutory Delusions, Referential Delusions, Grandiose Delusions, Erotomanic Delusions, Nihilistic Delusions, Somatic Delusions, Jealous Delusions\n[S020] Hallucinations: Perception-like experiences that occur without an external stimulus and are as vivid and clear as normal perception.  Subtypes: Auditory Hallucinations, Visual Hallucinations, Tactile Hallucinations, Olfactory Hallucinations, Gustatory Hallucinations\n[S021] Disorganized_Thinking_Speech: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.  Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking. Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. I mean, I didn’t even finish high school, just got straight to work, built my business from the ground up, and now I’m the CEO. But now, if somebody just walks past my office heading to the breakroom, I’m watching them, wondering what they're working on, and I've completely lost my place. \n\nAnd it’s not even just stuff happening around me, either. It’s my own head. I’ll be sitting there, trying to work, and suddenly I’ll have this totally random thought about my in-laws—like, some offhand comment my wife's dad made at a barbecue three weeks ago—and before I can even stop myself, I’m off down this mental rabbit hole and completely pulled off what I was doing. It just rips me right out of my work before I can even hit the brakes, and I just can't seem to get a handle on it lately.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected. Like I said, I got out of there as soon as I could and just started working. But looking back... yeah. Yeah, this actually was around when I was a kid, too. I mean, it was a whole thing. My teachers were just constantly, and I mean constantly, moving my seat. It was like a game of musical chairs but just for me. They’d put me in the back, then I’d be too distracting, so they’d move me right up to the front, literally touching the teacher's desk, thinking that would fix it. \n\nBut it didn't really matter where they put me, because I just... I talked way too much. I'd talk to the kid next to me, the kid behind me, whoever would listen, really. I just always had something to say. And I could never just stay put. I'd just get up without asking. I wouldn't even think about it, I'd just suddenly be out of my chair walking to the pencil sharpener or going to look at something on the bulletin board while the teacher was right in the middle of a lesson. They hated that. \n\nAnd the other thing that drove them absolutely up the wall was that I would just blurt things out. A teacher would be standing there, halfway through asking a question to the class, setting up a whole math problem or something, and before they could even finish getting the words out, I was already shouting out the answer. Or just a comment. Whatever popped into my head. It was like I couldn't hold it in for even a second to let them finish the sentence. So... yeah, I guess the more I think about it, I was definitely a handful back then with all that. It just felt like I had this motor running that I couldn't shut off.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep). Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. They started getting way too involved in how my wife and I are running our household, questioning my work hours, making these little passive-aggressive comments about how much time I spend at the office. Which, by the way, you don't become a CEO by working nine to five, and I've tried explaining this to them a hundred times, but it just doesn't compute. \n\nSo it created this constant, heavy tension. My wife is stressed out about it, which obviously stresses me out, and then I'll be sitting at my desk trying to look at a P&L statement, and instead of doing my job, my brain is just suddenly replaying some stupid argument we had in their driveway fourteen months ago. It’s just... it’s exhausting, honestly. It’s like the volume on all the distractions just got cranked all the way up right around that time, and I just haven't been able to tune any of it out since. It's just bleeding into my work, my headspace, everything.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. I just get so fed up with the sheer audacity of them coming into my house, drinking my wine, and making these passive-aggressive little jabs about how I'm on my phone too much. \n\nMy wife gets emotional about it, sure, because they're her parents, and I hate seeing her upset. I just want to be able to focus on the things that actually matter, like scaling my business and providing for my family, without this constant, ridiculous peanut gallery chiming in every five minutes and completely derailing my train of thought.
